# sv (StackVet) — what applies, and what is known

Produced by `sv` 0.1.0 (commit 082ca7feb89f) on 2026-10-22 20:42:01 UTC, run 44f4f1614a4d. ASVS level 3.

**This file:** Every requirement that applies, chapter by chapter, with what is known of each; the threats; what only you can check; and the tests worth writing. **For:** Anyone who needs the whole picture against the standards, you or someone you show it to. The end of `report.html` lists every file in this folder and who each is for.

## The short version

3570 things were found, worst first. 3258 of them are in test or sample code, about requirements this app is not held to, or from rules written for another language than the file's, listed after the app's own. 1 more is a note worth knowing, not a thing to fix, under "Worth knowing". 12 things in test code are information, not counted as findings: secrets.credential-assignment. Review them with `sv review`.

Nothing shows that sv was used while this app was built: no call from an AI coding tool to sv's MCP server is recorded for it. That is not a finding. The app may have been checked at a terminal, or built before sv kept this record.

This run took 50.0 s. The slowest parts: Running the outside tools, unless --no-tools (19.7 s), the outside tool Semgrep (19.7 s), Putting the report together (16.6 s), Reading its code (5.6 s), Reading its configuration (4.3 s).

The app's software bill of materials, every package it ships, is in sbom.cdx.json beside this report: 119 packages, not complete: some of the app's package lists could not be read, and the report says which, and 3 are listed at the version asked for, since no lockfile says which version is installed.

stackvet.sarif beside this report is for code editors and GitHub's code scanning, not for reading: it holds 3622 findings, and 27 notes on what was not examined.

Held to ASVS level 3. Not in these numbers: 5 not yet placed because nobody has answered the question that decides them. Level 3 because stackvet.toml asks for level 3 (`[app] level`), above the level 1 its other answers set: answers in stackvet.toml, which your AI coding tool usually writes and nobody has confirmed, so check them, and confirm them with `sv review`.

Not run this time: the running app, signed in or not (`sv report --run`). 98 of the requirements that apply can only be checked that way.

### What to do next

1. Fix the 312 things that were found. *(each is below, and in security.md with what to do)*
2. Answer the 76 questions at level 1 that no tool can settle — what your rules are, who may do what, how the app is built. 229 in all, most of them design review at higher levels. *(ask your AI coding tool to ask you them one at a time (it calls stackvet_check with section "questions" and records your answers); at a terminal, `sv questions` prints them)*
3. Ask your AI coding tool to write tests for the 150 requirements a test could settle, starting with the 18 at level 1. *("Tests to write", lowest level first)*
4. Run the full report, which starts the app behind sv's network fence and runs the outside tools: 98 requirements can only be checked that way. *(`sv report` does not start the app unless you pass --run.)*
5. Answer the questions in stackvet.toml that would place 5 more requirements, which are in none of the numbers above until then. *("Requirements nobody has placed" names the question for each)*

## How this report was made

414 requirements apply to this app. Of those, **35 have been looked at by something** and **379 have not been looked at at all**.

A further 63 about how the app is built with an AI coding tool, from OWASP AISVS Appendix C, are counted apart: see "How the app is built with AI", below.

There is no line in this report that says a requirement passed, because nothing here is able to establish that. A requirement marked *checked* had at least one automated check look at it and find nothing wrong, over the coverage named beside it — which is worth having and is not the same as the requirement being met. One marked as answered in the security notes, checked by hand, or answered yes rests on your word or your AI coding tool's, which is shown as exactly that and never as checked. Everything else applicable is *not verified*: nothing has produced evidence either way.

A further **5 requirements could not even be placed**: nobody has answered the question that decides whether they apply. They are listed at the end with the question in each case. They are not exclusions and they are not passes.

| | count |
|---|---:|
| Applies, needs attention | 33 |
| Applies, checked by an automated check | 1 |
| Applies, checked in part: an automated check tried some of what it asks | 1 |
| Applies, the app's own tests ran without failing: written by your AI coding tool, not a check of sv's | 0 |
| Applies, answered in the security notes: by you, or written by your AI coding tool and confirmed by a person | 0 |
| Applies, rests on your word: you checked it by hand | 0 |
| Applies, rests on your word: you answered yes about how it is built | 0 |
| Applies, rests on your AI coding tool's word: it answered yes | 0 |
| Applies, not verified by anything | 379 |
| Does not apply | 158 |
| Not assessed — nobody has answered | 5 |
| Above this app's target level (ASVS level 3) | 0 |

150 requirements do not apply only because stackvet.toml says no to `authorization-server`, `email`, `generates-media`, `jwt`, `mcp`, `multi-agent`, `multi-tenant`, `multimodal-ai`, `multiple-services`, `out-of-band-auth`, `out-of-band-or-email-code`, `self-hosted-model`, `shared-hostname`, `tls`, `training`, and `webrtc`. `sv` did not check those answers: stackvet.toml does not record who gave `authorization-server`, `email`, `generates-media`, `jwt`, `mcp`, `multi-agent`, `multi-tenant`, `multimodal-ai`, `multiple-services`, `out-of-band-auth`, `out-of-band-or-email-code`, `self-hosted-model`, `shared-hostname`, `tls`, `training`, and `webrtc`, so each is read as your AI coding tool's word, and finding nothing in the code does not show that an app lacks something. If one is wrong, change it to `true` and run `sv` again.

## What was not examined

Each of these is a limit on what the rest of this report can mean.

| not examined | why |
|---|---|
| the running app | `sv report` does not start the app unless you pass --run. Without it, nothing here has asked the app anything — what it sends to a browser, what it says when something goes wrong, which sites it accepts. |
| 1 folder left out as installed or built code | a folder named like an ecosystem's output, beside the file that makes it so, or a Python virtual environment under any name, holds code the app installed or built rather than wrote, and no check read it, which is normal: `target/` (output beside its Cargo.toml). If one holds the app's own code, move it or rename the folder. |
| what the folders named as not the app use | stackvet.toml says these folders are not the app (`[repository] not-the-app`): `crates/sv-check/tests`, `crates/sv-cli/tests`, `crates/sv-frameworks/tests`, `crates/sv-manifest/tests`, `crates/sv-report/tests`, `crates/sv-run/tests`, `crates/sv-scan/tests`, `docs`, `examples`, `tools`, holding 481 of the app's 1179 code files. Their code is still checked, and its findings count, listed with test and sample code. What is in them cannot change which requirements apply: a library an example uses is not one the app uses. If the app's own code is in one of them, take it off the list. |
| whether the app itself has `xml` | The only sign of it is docs/website/diagrams/source/make_diagrams.py, in a folder stackvet.toml says is not the app (`[repository] not-the-app`), so it is not counted as a "no": nothing else answers it, so its requirements wait on that question rather than being set aside. |

## Comments that tell a scanner to look away

Each of these comments in the app's code, or entries in a scanner's own ignore file, tells a security scanner to skip a line or a warning. An AI coding tool asked to make a finding go away may add one rather than fix the problem. Where a scanner StackVet runs can be told to look anyway, it is, so a finding a comment tried to hide is still among the things to fix when the scanner found it. Nothing here counts toward any requirement or credits anything. If you did not mean something to be hidden, take the comment out.

## How the app is built with AI (OWASP AISVS Appendix C)

63 requirements of OWASP AISVS Appendix C (AI-Assisted Secure Coding) apply to how this app is built with an AI coding tool, rather than to the app itself, and nothing has checked them. They are listed here rather than in the counts above. 35 are what the rules given to your AI coding tool come from (`sv rules`, or `stackvet_guidance` from inside the tool); the rules are instructions, and following them is not evidence that they are met. 3 are your decisions, and are among your questions. Nothing in `sv` reaches the other 25: most are about a CI pipeline or an organization's AI tooling. 3 more do not apply to this app, and are listed with the others that do not.

| requirement | what happens to it | what it asks for |
|---|---|---|
| AC.1.1 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that a written workflow says when AI tools may generate, refactor, or review code. The workflow names the approved tools, the prohibited use cases, and the data classifications that are allowed as input. |
| AC.1.2 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that the workflow covers every SSDLC phase from design and implementation through code review, testing, deployment, and post-deployment monitoring, and names the security gates that stay mandatory whether AI was involved or not. |
| AC.1.3 | nothing in `sv` reaches it | Verify that the workflow names the adversarial-AI threat scenarios it is built to mitigate. The list should cover prompt injection delivered through PR content, AI-generated supply-chain payloads, autonomous agents approving their own work, fork-PR secret exfiltration, and compromise of the model supply chain. |
| AC.1.4 | your decision, among your questions | Verify that metrics are collected on AI-produced and AI-mediated code, and that the results are compared against a human-only baseline. Vulnerability density, mean-time-to-detect, AI-attributable defect rate, prompt-injection detection rate, and fork-PR rejection rate are all useful. |
| AC.2.1 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that every AI tool, whether it is an assistant, a reviewer, an agent, or an MCP server, has a threat model. The threat model covers misuse, model inversion, training-data leakage, prompt injection from untrusted input, insecure output handling, excessive agency, and risk inherited from its dependency chain. |
| AC.2.2 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that the evaluation of each tool covers the local components (static and dynamic analysis), the SaaS endpoints (TLS, AuthN/AuthZ, logging, data residency), and the vendor's model supply chain (training-data provenance, fine-tune history, RAG sources). Each of these is reviewed and the review is written down. |
| AC.2.3 | nothing in `sv` reaches it | Verify that each tool goes through adversarial robustness testing before onboarding. The testing is repeated after any material change to the model or to the system prompts. Coverage includes automated prompt-injection probes, jailbreak suites, and indirect-injection corpora delivered through realistic PR and issue surfaces. |
| AC.2.4 | nothing in `sv` reaches it | Verify that evaluations follow a recognized framework such as NIST AI RMF, NIST AI 600-1 Generative AI Profile, or ISO/IEC 42001. Evaluations are repeated after a major version change, a vendor incident, or new threat intelligence relevant to the tool class. |
| AC.3.1 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that written guidance forbids putting secrets, credentials, PII, or classified data in any prompt sent to an AI tool. The guidance is enforced in pre-commit hooks, IDE integrations, and CI. |
| AC.3.2 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that technical controls automatically strip sensitive material from any context window sent to an AI tool. Client-side redaction, approved context filters, and secret scanners with pre-prompt hooks all qualify. |
| AC.3.3 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that any externally sourced context being fed to an AI tool is treated as untrusted and screened for prompt injection before it reaches the prompt. Sources to cover: PR descriptions and comments, fork-supplied diffs, issue bodies, commit messages, third-party documentation, web search results, and MCP tool outputs. |
| AC.3.4 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that the AI tool enforces an instruction hierarchy, with system and developer messages taking precedence over untrusted repository content. This hierarchy has to hold across multi-turn conversations and tool-augmented workflows. |
| AC.3.5 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that input length controls stop untrusted PR or repository content from crowding system instructions or safety directives out of the effective context window. Oversized inputs are rejected outright. Silent truncation is not acceptable. |
| AC.3.6 | nothing in `sv` reaches it | Verify that prompts and AI responses are encrypted in transit and at rest, and retained per the data-classification policy. Tenants and projects are cryptographically separated from each other. |
| AC.4.1 | your decision, among your questions | Verify that AI-generated code always goes through code review by a qualified human engineer. The reviewer must not be the same identity that asked for the AI generation in the first place (separation of duties). And the AI agent itself does not count as the human reviewer. |
| AC.4.2 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that automated security testing runs on every pull request containing AI-generated code: SAST, IAST, DAST, secret scanning, IaC scanning, and SCA. Where the scanner supports them, AI-attribution-aware rules are turned on. |

| AC.12.7 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that artifacts produced by untrusted PR workflows are treated as untrusted passive data when a privileged follow-up workflow consumes them. | AC.13.2 | given to your AI coding tool as a rule | Verify that PRs from first-time or low-reputation contributors require maintainer approval before any privileged workflow processes them. Privileged workflows here include AI review bots, secret-bearing jobs, and external-integration calls. |

## Threats

What could go wrong with an app like this one, by the part of it each threat concerns, and what the checks showed about each. These are the threats sv has rules for, for what was found in this app: not every threat there is. None is called handled. A threat is only as settled as the requirements that answer it, and each of those is one automated check at most, so the best a threat can be here is checked in part. Found threats come first, then those nothing has looked at.

Parts of the app: Browsers and the app; The app itself; The app's stored data; The AI model; Uploaded files; Programs using the app's API; Outside services; The documents the model searches; Live connections (WebSockets).

30 threats: 11 found, 19 not verified, 0 checked in part, 0 cannot place.

| threat | status | part of the app | what could happen | the requirements that answer it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T-04 (changing what should not change) | found | Browsers and the app | Crafted input (injection, a request forged from another site, oversized or unexpected fields) changes data or behavior. | needs attention: V15.3.3, V1.2.4, V1.2.1, V3.5.1; not verified: V2.2.1, V2.2.2, V3.4.3, V15.3.6 |
| T-27 (changing what should not change) | found | The app itself | A package the app depends on has a known vulnerability or a malicious update. | needs attention: V15.1.2; not verified: V15.2.1 |
| T-28 (exposing information) | found | The app itself | Secrets (session keys, encryption keys, API keys) leak through the code, the logs, or error pages. | needs attention: V13.3.1; not verified: V16.2.5, V13.4.1, V16.5.1 |
| T-25 (exposing information) | found | The app's stored data | Someone with access to the computer, or to a backup, copies the stored data and reads it. | needs attention: V11.4.2; not verified: V14.2.4 |
| T-09 (making the app unavailable) | found | The AI model | Runaway or abusive use of the model runs up the AI bill, spends tokens without limit, or exhausts the provider's quota. | needs attention: C9.1.2; not verified: C12.2.5, C9.6.1, C12.4.3, C11.2.2 |
| T-10 (pretending to be someone else) | found | The AI model | The AI provider's API key leaks, in logs, in a prompt, or in generated text, and someone else uses it. | needs attention: C9.5.4, V13.3.1; not verified: V13.3.2, V16.2.5 |
| T-12 (changing what should not change) | found | The AI model | The model is used to produce or spread harmful content. | needs attention: C2.2.1; not verified: C7.3.1 |
| T-13 (changing what should not change) | found | Uploaded files | A harmful file, such as a script disguised as an image or an SVG with code in it, is uploaded and later opened by someone else. | needs attention: V5.3.2; not verified: V5.2.2, V5.3.1, V5.4.1, V5.4.2 |

| T-14 (making the app unavailable) | not verified | Uploaded files | Oversized or endless uploads fill the disk. | not verified: V5.2.1, V2.3.2 |
| T-15 (exposing information) | not verified | Uploaded files | Someone downloads another person's file by guessing its address. | not verified: V8.2.2 |
| T-21 (making the app unavailable) | not verified | Programs using the app's API | A program hammers the API with requests. | not verified: V2.4.1 |
| T-23 (making the app unavailable) | not verified | Outside services | An outside service is slow or down and takes the app down with it. | not verified: V16.5.2 |
| T-35 (exposing information) | not verified | The documents the model searches | The model answers one person with documents another person may not see, because retrieval does not check who is asking. | not verified: C5.2.2, C8.1.3 |
| T-36 (changing what should not change) | not verified | The documents the model searches | Documents written into the store, including the model's own output, steer its later answers. | not verified: C8.2.3, C8.1.2 |
| T-37 (exposing information) | not verified | The documents the model searches | Sensitive fields are embedded into the store, where any search can bring them back. | not verified: C8.2.1 |
| T-39 (changing what should not change) | not verified | Live connections (WebSockets) | Another website opens a live connection to the app with the person's cookies and sends messages as them. | not verified: V4.4.2, V4.4.4 |
| T-40 (exposing information) | not verified | Live connections (WebSockets) | Messages on a live connection are read on the network. | not verified: V4.4.1 |

### For a security reviewer: these threats in MITRE ATLAS

The threats above that are about AI, as MITRE ATLAS names them (release 2026.09), for anyone who works from that catalog. These are references, not checks: nothing was tested against ATLAS, and they change no status above. MITRE ATLAS is a trademark of The MITRE Corporation; its data is used under the Apache License 2.0.

| threat | MITRE ATLAS technique | what the two share |
|---|---|---|
| T-09 | AML.T0034 Cost Harvesting | running up the bill: the cost, harvesting it |
| T-09 | AML.T0029 Denial of AI Service | exhausting the provider's quota, a denial of the service |
| T-10 | AML.T0055 Unsecured Credentials | a leaked API key is an unsecured credential |
| T-12 | AML.T0048 External Harms | harmful content spread to others, external harms |
| T-07 | AML.T0051 LLM Prompt Injection | prompt injection |
| T-08 | AML.T0057 LLM Data Leakage | data leakage of other people's records |
| T-08 | AML.T0056 Extract LLM System Prompt | revealing its own instructions, the system prompt |
| T-11 | AML.T0053 AI Agent Tool Invocation | the model makes a change through an agent tool invocation |

## What only you can check

105 of the requirements that apply cannot be settled by any tool: they ask what your rules are, how the app is built, or what is true of it in production. Each one below says what doing something about it involves. None of them is counted as met — doing the thing is what would change that, not reading it here.

A further 187 are the Secure by Design and AISVS design-review controls, which are not listed one by one: those standards are checklists already, and repeating them here would be another wall of text. They are in the table above, and in the standards themselves.

| requirement | what to do | how |
|---|---|---|
| V2.1.1 — What counts as valid input | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | For each thing people type into the app (a name, an email address, a date, an amount), what does a good one look like, how long may it be, and what should the app do with one that is wrong? In the standard's words: For each kind of information people type into the app (names, email addresses, dates, amounts, free text), write down the validation rules: what a valid value looks like, how long it may be, and what the app does with one that is not valid. **Where to look:** Go through every form and every field an API accepts, and for each one write what a good value looks like. The quickest way to find them all is to open each page that has a form and list its inputs. |
| V2.1.2 — Data items that must make sense combined | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which things people enter have to make sense together (a start date before an end date, say), and how does the app check them? In the standard's words: Where two or more data items have to agree with each other once combined (a start date before an end date, a city that matches the ZIP code), write down which combinations matter and how the app checks that logical consistency. **Where to look:** Open each form that takes more than one value and list the pairs that have to agree — two dates, a country and a postal code, a quantity and a maximum. For each, try the combination that should be impossible and see whether the app takes it. |
| V2.1.3 — Business limits | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | How many of something may one person make or do, and in how long? And is there any limit for the whole app, such as a total per day? In the standard's words: Write down the business logic limits: how many of something one person may create or do, and in how long (per user), and any limits across the whole app (globally), such as a daily total. **Where to look:** Go through the app as a user and note everything somebody could do repeatedly: create a record, send an invitation, start an export. For each, decide the number that would be too many in a day, and write that down. **In the code**, the brakes the code shows: timeouts, limits on size, rate, or rows, and work handed to a queue: docs/prompts/loop-compare/blind_tester.py, line 66; docs/prompts/loop-compare/blind_tester.py, line 81; docs/prompts/loop-pilot/loop_trial.py, line 60; and 12 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V5.1.1 — What files people may upload | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Where people can upload a file: which kinds of file are allowed, how big may one be (unpacked, for a zip), and what does the app do with a file that looks dangerous? In the standard's words: For each place people can upload a file: the permitted file types and extensions, the maximum size (including once unpacked, for a zip), and how the app makes a file safe to download again, including what happens when a file looks malicious. **Where to look:** Open each place the app takes a file and look at what the code accepts today: the extensions it allows, the size limit if there is one, and where the file ends up. Write down what it should be, which may not be what it is. |
| V6.1.1 — How sign-in is protected against guessing | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | After how many wrong passwords in a row does the app slow down or lock the account, and for how long? And how does it stop somebody locking a real person out on purpose? (Say the numbers you want, even if the app does not do it yet.) In the standard's words: How the app defends against someone trying many passwords (password brute force) or leaked email and password pairs (credential stuffing): rate limiting, delays, or lockout, and the numbers, such as five failed attempts in fifteen minutes. Say how these controls are configured, and how the app stops an attacker locking a real person out on purpose (malicious account lockout). **Where to look:** Look in your sign-in code for anything that counts failures — a rate limiter, a lockout, a delay. If you find nothing, that is the answer, and the number to write down is the one you want rather than the one you have. **In the code**, the brakes the code shows: timeouts, limits on size, rate, or rows, and work handed to a queue: docs/prompts/loop-compare/blind_tester.py, line 66; docs/prompts/loop-compare/blind_tester.py, line 81; docs/prompts/loop-pilot/loop_trial.py, line 60; and 12 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V6.1.2 — Words not allowed in passwords | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which words should nobody be allowed to use in a password here, because they are the first anyone would guess (the app's name, your organization's name, your products)? In the standard's words: A list of context-specific words people may not use in their passwords: the app's name, the organization's name, product names, and other words someone would guess first. **Where to look:** Start from the names somebody could guess without knowing you: the app, your organization, your product, the town you are in. Add the obvious variations with numbers on the end. |
| V6.1.3 — Every way to sign in | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | What are all the ways someone can sign in (a password, a Google or Microsoft account, a code by email, a key for programs), and are they all protected as well as each other? In the standard's words: If there is more than one way to sign in (a password, a Google or Microsoft account, a code by email, an API key), list all these authentication pathways, and say which security controls apply to each, so they are enforced consistently and one is not weaker than the others. **Where to look:** Open the sign-in page and list every button on it, then search the code for anywhere else a session is created — an API key check, a magic link, a mobile app, an admin back door. Each one is a pathway, and they should all be as strong as each other. |
| V7.1.1 — How long someone stays signed in | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | How long may someone be away before they have to sign in again, how long may anyone stay signed in at most, and why are those times right for this app? In the standard's words: The session inactivity timeout (how long someone can be away before signing in again) and the absolute maximum session lifetime (how long any session lasts at most), and why those times are right for this app. If they are longer than NIST SP 800-63B suggests, say why. **Where to look:** Find the two numbers in your app's configuration: how long an idle session lasts, and how long any session lasts at most. If you cannot find them, the answer is whatever your framework does by default, and that is the number to write down and then reconsider. **In the code**, where the code sets how long a session lasts: a session cookie's age, a session lifetime, or a sign-in token's expiry: crates/sv-run/assets/oidc-provider.mjs, line 181. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V7.1.2 — Signed in on several devices at once | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | How many devices may one person be signed in on at once, and what happens when they sign in on one more? In the standard's words: How many concurrent sessions one account may have at once (a phone and a laptop, say), and what the app does when someone reaches the maximum: sign out the oldest, refuse the new one, or tell the person. **Where to look:** Sign in to the same account from two browsers and see whether both keep working. That tells you what the app does today; write down what it should do. |
| V7.1.3 — Sign-in shared with other systems | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | If people sign in through another system (single sign-on), which systems keep them signed in, and how do they agree on when someone is signed out? In the standard's words: If people sign in through a shared system (single sign-on, SSO, a federated identity provider), list every system that creates or manages their sessions, and how session lifetimes and signing out are coordinated between them, including when to ask for re-authentication. **Where to look:** Only if people sign in through a shared system. List every system that can create a session for your app, and find out what happens in each when somebody signs out of one: often nothing, which is the thing to write down. |
| V8.1.1 — Who may do what | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Who may do what in the app: which kinds of people may use which features, and whose records may each one see or change? In the standard's words: The authorization rules: which kinds of users may use which functions (function-level: only administrators may open the admin page), and which records each may see or change (data-specific: a person may only open their own notes). **Where to look:** List the kinds of user the app has, then walk through the app as each one and note what they can reach. The pages that only some people should see, and the records that belong to one person, are what these rules are about. **In the code**, comparisons and queries of a record's owner or user id, where the app may keep a person to their own records; places that refuse a request with a 403 (Forbidden), whether the answer is sent, returned, or raised: docs/paper/tables/period_records.py, line 464; examples/notes-with-users/app.py, line 209; docs/prompts/loop-2/scripted_review.py, line 87; and 6 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V8.1.2 — Who may see or change each field | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Where some parts of a record are more private than the rest: who may see each part, who may change it, and does that change once the record is, say, published? In the standard's words: Where some fields of a record are more restricted than the record itself, the field-level access rules: who may read and who may write each field, and whether that depends on the record's state or status (a published order's total can no longer be changed). **Where to look:** Take a record with a field not everybody should see — a price, a note, an internal status. Write down who may read it and who may change it, and whether that depends on the record's state. **In the code**, places the code chooses which fields of a record are read, written, or sent: a serializer's field list, a query that selects some columns, and a model's own list of what it shows: crates/sv-run/assets/browser-driver.mjs, line 178; docs/prompts/loop-2/scripted_review.py, line 48. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V11.1.1 — How keys are looked after | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | For each secret key the app uses: where is it kept, who can get to it, how often is it replaced, and what happens if it may have leaked? In the standard's words: The policy for cryptographic keys: where each key is kept, who can reach it, how often it is replaced, and what happens when one may have leaked, following a key management standard such as NIST SP 800-57 through each key's lifecycle. **Where to look:** List every key the app uses: session signing, encryption at rest, the TLS certificate, anything a provider gave you. For each, write where it lives, who can read it, and what you would do tomorrow if it leaked. |
| V11.1.2 — Every key, algorithm, and certificate | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | What is every key, kind of encryption, and certificate the app uses, and what may each one be used for? In the standard's words: A cryptographic inventory: every key, algorithm, and certificate the app uses, where each key may and may not be used, and what data each may and may not protect. Keep it updated when one is added or replaced. **Where to look:** Look in your .env and your host's secret store, at the certificate on the live site, and at whatever the code calls to hash or encrypt. That is the inventory; write down which data each protects and where it may not be used. |
| V13.1.1 — Everything the app talks to | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | What does the app connect to outside itself (payments, email, AI services, other programs), and why? And can anyone using the app give it an address that it will then visit itself? In the standard's words: All the communication needs of the app: every external service it connects to (payment providers, email, AI services, other APIs) and why, and any place where someone using the app can give it an address that the app will then connect to itself. **Where to look:** Look at your dependency list for anything that calls out — payment, email, AI, maps, analytics — and at the report's own list of outside services. Then add the places where somebody using the app can give it an address it will fetch. **In the code**, web addresses written into the code: some are services the app connects to, and some are only links or the app's own address: crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep-aisvs/app/assistant.py, line 48; crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep/app/app.py, line 52; crates/sv-run/assets/oidc-provider.mjs, line 43; and 13 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V14.1.2 — How each kind of sensitive data is protected | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | For each kind of private information the app keeps: is it encrypted, how long is it kept, may it appear in logs, who can see it, and what does the privacy law you follow require? In the standard's words: For each kind of sensitive data the app holds, its protection requirements: whether it is encrypted, how long it is kept (retention), whether and how it may appear in logs, who can see it, and anything the privacy law you follow requires. **Where to look:** Take each kind of data from the list you made for V14.1.1 and decide four things for it: encrypted or not, kept for how long, allowed in logs or not, and who may see it. |
| V15.1.1 — How quickly outdated libraries are updated | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | When a library the app uses turns out to have a security hole, how soon must it be updated, by how serious the hole is (critical within a week, say)? And how often are libraries updated anyway? In the standard's words: Time frames for remediation: how soon a third-party library with a known vulnerability is updated, by how serious the vulnerability is (for example, critical within a week), and how often libraries are updated in general. **Where to look:** Decide the number of days you would accept between a vulnerability being published and it being fixed, for each level of severity. Start from how bad it would be for this app rather than from what other people write down. Then put the same numbers in stackvet.toml as `[policy] fix-within-days`, so `sv audit` can hold the app's packages to them. |
| V15.1.3 — Slow or heavy features | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which features are slow or heavy (big exports, reports, AI requests, image processing), and what stops one person's heavy use from making the app unavailable to everyone else? In the standard's words: Which functionality is time-consuming or resource-demanding (large exports, report generation, AI requests, image processing), and how the app prevents a loss of availability from overusing it: queues, limits per user, timeouts. **Where to look:** Time the slow things: an export, a report, a search over everything, an AI request, anything that resizes an image. Anything a person notices waiting for is what this asks about. **In the code**, work that can be slow or heavy: password hashing, images and PDFs, archives, exports, calls to an AI model, and files uploaded; the brakes the code shows: timeouts, limits on size, rate, or rows, and work handed to a queue: crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep-aisvs/app/assistant.js, line 13; docs/prompts/loop-compare/blind_tester.py, line 66; crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep-aisvs/app/assistant.js, line 25; and 22 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V16.1.1 — What the app logs | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | What does the app write in its logs, where are they kept, who can read them, what are they for, and how long are they kept? In the standard's words: An inventory of the logging: which events are logged, in what format, where the logs are stored, who can read them (access), what they are used for, and how long they are kept. **Where to look:** Find where the app's output actually goes — the console, a file, your host's logging, an outside service — and what it writes at each level. Then say who can read it and how long it is kept. |
| V3.1.1 — Which browser security features the app expects, and what it does without them | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which safety features must a visitor's browser have to use the app (a secure connection, for one), and what does the app do when a browser lacks one: warn the person, or keep them out? In the standard's words: Write down the security features a browser must support to use the app safely (such as HTTPS, HTTP Strict Transport Security, and a Content Security Policy), and how the application behaves when a browser does not support one of them: does it warn the user, or block access? **Where to look:** List the security headers the app sends (an `sv report` run against the app lists them), then decide, for an old or unusual browser that ignores one, whether the app should carry on, show a warning, or refuse. |
| V8.1.3 — The time, place, address, or device the app looks at to decide | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Does the app ever decide something about safety from the time of day, where someone is, the internet address they come from, or the device they use, and if so, which of those does it look at, and for what? In the standard's words: Write down the environmental and contextual attributes the application uses to make security decisions, such as the time of day, the user's location, their IP address, or their device, and which decisions each one is used in, including those about authentication and authorization. **Where to look:** Search the code for where it reads the time, the visitor's address, their location, or their browser, and note each place that changes what someone may do or whether they must sign in again. |
| V8.1.4 — How those factors change a sign-in or a permission: thresholds and actions | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | When the app judges a sign-in or a request riskier than usual, what makes it riskier, how risky is too risky, and what happens then: let it through, ask again, refuse, or ask for a second proof of who they are? In the standard's words: In the authentication and authorization documentation, write down how environmental and contextual factors are used in decision-making, beside the function-level, data-specific, and field-level rules: the attributes evaluated, the thresholds for risk, and the action taken at each (allow, challenge, deny, or step-up authentication). **Where to look:** Start from the list written for V8.1.3. For each factor, write the point at which it changes the outcome and what the app does then. If nothing changes the outcome, say that: it is an answer. |
| V11.1.4 — A plan for moving to new cryptographic standards | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | If the ways the app scrambles and signs data had to be swapped for newer ones (as quantum computers may one day require), is there a written plan for what would change, in what order, and who decides? In the standard's words: Keep the cryptographic inventory, and with it a documented plan for the migration path to new cryptographic standards, such as post-quantum cryptography, so the app can react to future threats: what would be replaced, in what order, and who decides when. **Where to look:** Start from the list of cryptography written for V11.1.2. For each entry, note what would replace it and whether the library the app uses already offers the replacement. |
| V13.1.2 — How many connections to each service, and what happens at the limit | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | For each service the app connects to (its database, for one), how many connections can be open at once, and what does the app do when they are all in use: wait, give up, or fall back to something else? In the standard's words: For each service the application uses, write down the maximum number of concurrent connections (for example, a database connection pool's limit), and how the application behaves when that limit is reached, including any fallback or recovery, so the limit does not become a denial of service. **Where to look:** Look in the app's settings for a pool size or a connection limit for each service, often in the database settings or the code that connects. Where none is set, the library's default is the limit; write down which. |
| V13.1.3 — Timeouts, retries, and releasing what each outside system hands the app | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | For each outside system the app uses, how long does the app wait before giving up, does it try again and how many times, and does it always let go of what it opened (a file, a connection) when it is done or something fails? In the standard's words: Write down the resource-management strategy for every external system or service the app uses (databases, file handles, threads, HTTP connections): how each resource is released, the timeout settings, failure handling, and where retry logic exists, its retry limits, delays, and back-off. For a synchronous HTTP request, the timeout should be short, and retries off or strictly limited, so delays do not cascade. **Where to look:** Find each place the app calls a database, another website's API, or opens a file, and look for a timeout and a retry setting beside it. A call with no timeout waits for ever by default in many libraries; write that down too. |
| V13.1.4 — Which secrets matter most, and when each is rotated | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which of the app's passwords and keys would do the most harm if someone else had them, and how often is each one replaced with a new one? In the standard's words: Write down the secrets that are critical for the security of the application (keys, passwords to other services, signing secrets) and a schedule for rotating each, based on the organization's threat model and business requirements. **Where to look:** List what the app reads from its environment or its secrets store: the database password, other services' keys, the key that signs sign-in cookies. For each, write who can replace it, how, and how often. |
| V15.1.4 — The third-party libraries considered risky components | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Of the add-on packages the app uses, which ones would you call risky (no longer looked after, handling what strangers send, or hard to replace), and why? In the standard's words: In the application documentation, highlight the third-party libraries considered to be "risky components": ones that are unmaintained, that run with more access than they need, that parse untrusted input, or that the app could not easily replace. **Where to look:** Go through the dependency list (package.json, requirements.txt, or the like). For each package, look at when it last had a release and what it does with what visitors send. `sv report` lists the known vulnerabilities in each when given the advisory data. |
| V15.1.5 — The parts of the app with dangerous functionality | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it writes it in security-notes.md (at a terminal, `sv notes` makes the file) | Which parts of the app do something that could go badly wrong if misused (running another program, reading uploaded files, building a file path or a database query from what someone typed), and where are they? In the standard's words: In the application documentation, highlight the parts of the application where "dangerous functionality" is used: running other programs, building database queries or file paths from what a user sends, reading uploaded files, or turning stored data back into objects. **Where to look:** Search the code for where it runs a command, opens a file by a name that came from a request, builds a query by joining text, or loads stored data as objects. Each place you find is one to list. |
| V2.2.2 — Input validation is enforced at a trusted service layer | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it records it in the `[design]` section of stackvet.toml | When someone types something into a form, does the server check it before using it, and not only the page in their browser (which anyone can skip)? In the standard's words: Does the application enforce input validation on the server — a trusted service layer — and not only in the browser? Client-side validation improves usability and should be kept, but anyone can skip it by sending a request straight to the server, so it settles nothing on its own. **Where to look:** Find where the app receives a form or an API request, and look at what happens to the values before they are used. If the only checks you find are in the browser — HTML attributes, JavaScript — that is the answer. |
| V8.3.1 — Authorization rules are enforced at a trusted service layer | tell your AI coding tool your answer, and it records it in the `[design]` section of stackvet.toml | When somebody tries to open a page or do something they are not allowed to, is it the server that stops them, or only a button that is hidden? In the standard's words: Does the application enforce its authorization rules at a trusted service layer — the server — rather than relying on controls an untrusted consumer could manipulate, such as a hidden button or a disabled field in the browser? Hiding a link is not a rule: anyone can request the address directly. **Where to look:** Take a page or an action only some people should reach, and find the code that stops everybody else. | V2.3.1 — Steps cannot be skipped or done out of order | check it by hand; nothing here can | Take a flow with more than one step — checkout, sign-up with a confirmation, anything with a "next" button. Then try the steps in the wrong order, and try the same step twice. Describing the flow in stackvet.toml as `flow` under `[stack.run.users]` lets `sv report --run` try two skips, one wrong order, and one repeated step for you; other orders, and the last step sent again after the flow finished, are still yours. |

Without the numbers it counts every known vulnerability as late, whatever its age. |

| C11.1.1 — The AI model has had safety training | check it by hand; nothing here can | Look up which model the app uses (your AI coding tool can tell you where it is named) and open the provider's model card or safety documentation. Check that it describes alignment or safety training against disallowed content. Models from the major providers do; a model you downloaded or trained yourself may not, and then this is yours to arrange. |
| C11.1.2 — The AI's safety is tested again whenever the model changes | check it by hand; nothing here can | Write down a short list of prompts the app must refuse or answer carefully, and keep it in version control beside the code. Each time the model is updated or its version changes, run the list again and compare the answers. Check whether this happens today; if nobody reruns anything when the model changes, the answer is no. |
| C11.1.3 — The AI has been tried against known attacks | check it by hand; nothing here can | Try the well-known attacks on the AI part of your app yourself: ask it to ignore its instructions, to reveal its system prompt, to role-play its way past a rule, or hide an instruction inside a document or image it reads. Write down which ones it resisted. A tool such as garak runs many of these adversarial techniques at once. |
| C11.1.4 — The AI is hardened against tricky inputs | check it by hand; nothing here can | Take the attacks from the item above that worked, and check what the app now does about them: input it filters, instructions it keeps apart from what a visitor types, answers it checks before showing. Try each attack again. Hardened means each one that worked before now fails. |
| C11.3.1 — Someone copying the AI by asking it everything would be noticed | check it by hand; nothing here can | Look at what the app records about the questions people ask the AI: who asked, how many, how fast. Check whether anything watches that record for the pattern of someone systematically asking thousands of varied questions to copy what the model knows, the query pattern of an extraction attempt, and warns somebody. |
| C12.2.2 — Unusual behavior toward the AI is spotted | check it by hand; nothing here can | Check whether the app watches for conversations that look like probing: the same question retried many times with small changes, excessive retry attempts, or one visitor trying many different tricks in a row. Try it yourself and look for whether anything was flagged or anyone told. |
| C12.2.3 — Jailbreak and prompt-injection attempts are recognized | check it by hand; nothing here can | List the rules the app uses to recognize attacks aimed at the AI: jailbreak phrasings, prompt injection hidden in documents it reads, attempts to extract its system prompt. Try a few coordinated attempts from two accounts at once, and check whether the rules catch them and something is recorded. |
| AC.4.1 — A person reviews the code the AI wrote | check it by hand; nothing here can | Look at how changes to the app get in: check that every change your AI coding tool wrote is read and approved by a qualified person before it goes live, and that the reviewer is not the same person who asked the AI for the code. The AI agent does not count as the human reviewer. If changes go straight from the AI to the live app, the answer is no. |
| AC.6.3 — The AI coding tools themselves are attacked on purpose, on a schedule | check it by hand; nothing here can | Check whether anyone regularly tries to trick the AI tools that help build the app: a pull request, issue, or comment with a hidden instruction, a known jailbreak, or a request to add a harmful package. Write down when this was last done and what it found, and whether each finding was tracked until fixed. If it has never been done, schedule the first red-team exercise. |
| V2.2.1 — Input is checked against what the app expects | check it by hand; nothing here can | List the places the app takes something typed or sent in that decides what happens next: an amount, a quantity, a date, a status, a role, a choice from a list. For each, try a value it should refuse: a negative or huge number, a date in the past, a word that is not one of the choices, an empty field. The app should refuse it with a message, not accept it and carry on. A form that only checks in the browser does not count: send the same value again with the browser's tools, or ask your AI coding tool to show you the check on the server. |
| V1.3.3 — Input is cleaned before it reaches a dangerous place | check it by hand; nothing here can | Ask your AI coding tool to list every place the app passes something a person typed into another program: a shell command, a file path, an email header, a spreadsheet export, a search sent elsewhere. For each, look at what it does to the input first. It should allow only the characters that place can safely take, and cut off input that is too long. Then try one: put a semicolon, a quote mark, or a very long line into that field, and check the result is refused or harmless. **In the code**, places the app hands a value to another program or format: a shell command, a database query, a file sent back, an email, or a spreadsheet row: crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep/app/app.py, line 22; crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/semgrep/app/app.py, line 27; crates/sv-check/tests/fixtures/suppressed/bandit-app/blanket.py, line 2; and 78 more. These are where to look, not evidence: StackVet found them by their names or their shape (a 403 sent, a query by owner) and did not read what they do. |
| V1.3.5 — Text people write cannot run as code in the page | check it by hand; nothing here can | If people can write formatted text the app shows to others (Markdown, a comment box, a profile with styling, uploaded stylesheets), try writing a script inside it: a Markdown link to `javascript:alert(1)`, or an HTML `<script>` tag inside the text. Open the page where it is shown, as another user. Nothing should pop up, and the raw tags should be shown as text or removed. If the app takes no formatted text from people, write that down; it does not apply. |
| V6.5.2 — Backup sign-in codes are stored scrambled, not as written | check it by hand; nothing here can | If the app gives people backup or recovery codes to sign in with, ask your AI coding tool to show you how they are stored. Open the database (or have the tool show you a row) and look at a stored code. It should be a long scrambled value made by a password hashing function such as bcrypt or Argon2, never the code itself. Try signing in with a code the app gave you: it should still work, which shows the scrambled value is checked rather than compared as written. |
| V6.5.3 — Sign-in codes come from an unpredictable random source | check it by hand; nothing here can | Ask your AI coding tool to show you the line that makes each sign-in code, reset code, backup code, and two-factor secret. It should use the language's secure random source (`secrets` in Python, `crypto.randomInt` or `crypto.randomBytes` in JavaScript, `SecureRandom` in Java, `crypto/rand` in Go). `random`, `Math.random`, or a code made from the time or the user's id is the wrong answer. `sv check` finds the commonest wrong ones itself; this is for any it did not. |
| V13.3.2 — Each part of the app can reach only the secrets it needs | check it by hand; nothing here can | List where the app's keys and passwords live (a hosting dashboard, a secrets manager, `.env` files, CI settings) and which parts of the app read each one. Check that each service, job, and person has access only to the ones it uses: the website should not hold the payment admin key, a test job should not see production keys, and a database login should not be an administrator unless it must be. `sv check` finds a CI workflow that hands every secret to a job; this covers the rest. |
| V16.3.4 — Unexpected errors and failed security checks are logged | check it by hand; nothing here can | Open the app's logs where they are kept (the hosting dashboard, a log service, or the terminal it runs in). Then cause a few failures: stop the database briefly or give it a wrong password, send a request the app should refuse, and visit an address that makes it fail. Each should leave a line in the log saying what went wrong and when, without a password or key in it. Failures that leave no trace are the wrong answer. |
| C2.1.1 — Text is put into one standard form before the model reads it | check it by hand; nothing here can | Ask your AI coding tool to show you what the app does to a person's text before it is sent to the model or turned into an embedding. Look for a step that puts the text into one standard form (Unicode normalization, such as NFKC) so that look-alike characters and invisible ones become plain text. If the text goes straight to the model as typed, that is the wrong answer. |
| C2.1.2 — Hidden or disguised instructions in input are caught | check it by hand; nothing here can | Try sending the AI feature an instruction disguised so a simple filter would miss it: written in Base64, split by invisible characters, in letters from another alphabet that look like English, or inside an HTML comment. Check what the app does. It should refuse, mark the text as untrusted, or strip the disguise before the model sees it, and never follow the hidden instruction. |
| C2.1.4 — Input that is too long is refused, not cut short | check it by hand; nothing here can | Try pasting a very long text into the AI feature, longer than the model can take (several hundred pages of text). The app should refuse it with a message saying it is too long. Cutting it short quietly and carrying on is the wrong answer, because the part cut off can be the part that mattered, such as the instructions at the end. |
| C2.1.5 — Input to the AI feature is held to the characters it needs | check it by hand; nothing here can | Ask your AI coding tool to show you the list of characters the AI feature accepts. It should be a list of what is allowed (letters, numbers, ordinary punctuation, the languages your users write in), not a list of what is blocked. Then try sending control characters, emoji sequences, or invisible characters, and check they are refused or removed. |
| C2.2.2 — Content screening works in other languages too | check it by hand; nothing here can | Take the harmful prompt you tried for the content screening and translate it into a language your app does not expect, such as one written in a different alphabet. Send it. The app should refuse it or mark it as not screened, rather than let it through because the screening only understood English. |
| C7.4.1 — Answers built from documents say which documents | check it by hand; nothing here can | If the AI feature answers from your own documents or records (retrieval, often called RAG), ask it a question whose answer is in one of them. The answer should name or link the documents it came from. An answer with no sources, or with sources you cannot open, is the wrong answer. |
| C7.4.2 — The listed sources come from the search, not from the model | check it by hand; nothing here can | Ask your AI coding tool to show you where the source list under each answer is made. It should come from the records the search actually returned (their titles, ids, or addresses), added by the app. If the model is asked to write its own sources, try asking it to cite a document that does not exist: a made-up source appearing in the list is the wrong answer. |
| C9.1.1 — Each tool the AI uses has limits on time and resources | check it by hand; nothing here can | List the tools the AI feature can use (running code, fetching pages, reading files, querying the database). For each, ask your AI coding tool to show you its limits: how long it may run, how much memory and disk it may use, and where it may connect. Then ask the AI to do something that never ends, such as fetching a page that never answers or running an endless loop. It should be stopped after the limit, not left running. |
| C9.2.1 — High-impact actions wait for a person to approve them | check it by hand; nothing here can | List what the AI feature can do that is hard to undo: sending money, deleting records, emailing customers, changing permissions. Ask it to do each one. The app should stop and ask a person to approve first, and the approval should be checked by the app itself, not just written in the chat. The action going ahead without a click from a person is the wrong answer. |
| C10.2.2 — The MCP server checks every access token fully | check it by hand; nothing here can | If your app runs its own MCP server that other tools sign in to, ask your AI coding tool to show you where it checks the access token each request presents. It should check who issued it (issuer), who it is for (audience), that it has not expired (expiration), and what it allows (scope). Then try a token for a different service, an expired one, and one with too little scope: each should be refused. |
| C10.2.3 — The MCP server does not keep access tokens or passwords | check it by hand; nothing here can | If your app runs its own MCP server, ask your AI coding tool to show you everything it stores: database tables, files, logs, caches. Search them for access tokens, refresh tokens, and passwords. The server should check each token and then forget it; finding tokens or user credentials stored anywhere is the wrong answer. |
| C10.3.2 — Local-only MCP connections stay on your own computer | check it by hand; nothing here can | List the MCP servers set up to run as a local program (the settings show a command to start, rather than an address). Check that each runs only on your own computer or in a controlled environment you set up, never on a shared server that other people or the internet can reach. Anything that needs to be shared should use an encrypted, signed-in connection instead. |
| V11.2.1 — Cryptography comes from a well-known library | check it by hand; nothing here can | Look at every place the app encrypts, decrypts, signs, hashes, or makes a key, and check that each one calls a well-known, industry-validated library (such as the language's own crypto module, OpenSSL, libsodium, or the cryptography package) rather than code the app or its AI coding tool wrote itself. A function named encrypt that shuffles or XORs bytes by hand is the thing to look for. Write down the library each place uses. |
| V3.7.5 — The app behaves as written when a browser lacks a security feature | check it by hand; nothing here can | Open what the security notes say for V3.1.1: the browser features the app expects, and what it does without one. Then try each: open the app in an old browser, or with JavaScript or cookies turned off, and check it warns or blocks just as written. |
| V13.2.6 — Each connection follows the settings written for it | check it by hand; nothing here can | Put the security notes for V13.1.2 and V13.1.3 (connection limits, timeouts, and retries for each service) beside the app's connection settings: its database pool, and its timeout and retry settings for each outside call. Check each one matches what was written down. |
| AC.1.4 — Metrics compare AI-written code with a human-only baseline | check it by hand; nothing here can | List the metrics the team collects on code an AI tool wrote or helped with: defects found later, vulnerabilities per thousand lines, how long problems take to find. Check each is compared with the same metric for code people wrote alone. If none are collected, write that down. |

## What the AI coding tool did that is worth a look

Information only: nothing here changes a requirement's status, counts toward anything, or is a finding. Each line is something the AI coding tool did that a person may want to look at, with when and where it was seen. Many of them are right to do; a line says what happened, not that it was wrong.

Read: the last 20 commits, as there is no earlier report made at a known commit, of which 20 were made by an AI coding tool, by a trailer in its message that names one; the 0 that name none are not read as the tool's.

Not looked for: findings that went away, since this folder held no earlier report that sv can show it wrote.

## Tests worth writing first

Nothing produced evidence about these, and no test in the app names them. A test that names a requirement's id and passes is the one way to give evidence about any requirement, including the ones no check here can reach. These are the level 1 ones. Name only what a test really checks: nothing here can tell whether it does.

132 more are at level 2 and above. They are not listed here, because a list that long is not something a person works through; `sv mcp` gives the whole list to your AI coding tool, and report.json carries it under `tests_to_write`.

158 more have no evidence and are not listed at all, because an application's own tests cannot show them: they ask for documentation, a deployment setting, a development process, or a design decision, and a person answers them.

| V3.4.2 | Verify that the Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS) Access-Control-Allow-Origin header field is a fixed value by the application, or if the Origin HTTP request header field value is used, it is validated against an allowlist of trusted origins. When 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *' needs to be used, verify that the response does not include any sensitive information. |
| V3.5.3 | Verify that HTTP requests to sensitive functionality use appropriate HTTP methods such as POST, PUT, PATCH, or DELETE, and not methods defined by the HTTP specification as "safe" such as HEAD, OPTIONS, or GET. Alternatively, strict validation of the Sec-Fetch-* request header fields can be used to ensure that the request did not originate from an inappropriate cross-origin call, a navigation request, or a resource load (such as an image source) where this is not expected. |
| V5.2.2 | Verify that when the application accepts a file, either on its own or within an archive such as a zip file, it checks if the file extension matches an expected file extension and validates that the contents correspond to the type represented by the extension. This includes, but is not limited to, checking the initial 'magic bytes', performing image re-writing, and using specialized libraries for file content validation. For L1, this can focus just on files which are used to make specific business or security decisions. For L2 and up, this must apply to all files being accepted. |
| V5.3.1 | Verify that files uploaded or generated by untrusted input and stored in a public folder, are not executed as server-side program code when accessed directly with an HTTP request. |
| V6.2.2 | Verify that users can change their password. |
| V6.2.3 | Verify that password change functionality requires the user's current and new password. |
| V6.2.5 | Verify that passwords of any composition can be used, without rules limiting the type of characters permitted. There must be no requirement for a minimum number of upper or lower case characters, numbers, or special characters. |
| V6.2.6 | Verify that password input fields use type=password to mask the entry. Applications may allow the user to temporarily view the entire masked password, or the last typed character of the password. |
| V6.2.7 | Verify that "paste" functionality, browser password helpers, and external password managers are permitted. |
| V6.2.8 | Verify that the application verifies the user's password exactly as received from the user, without any modifications such as truncation or case transformation. |
| V6.3.2 | Verify that default user accounts (e.g., "root", "admin", or "sa") are not present in the application or are disabled. |
| V6.4.1 | Verify that system generated initial passwords or activation codes are securely randomly generated, follow the existing password policy, and expire after a short period of time or after they are initially used. These initial secrets must not be permitted to become the long term password. |
| V7.2.1 | Verify that the application performs all session token verification using a trusted, backend service. |
| V7.2.2 | Verify that the application uses either self-contained or reference tokens that are dynamically generated for session management, i.e. not using static API secrets and keys. |
| V7.2.4 | Verify that the application generates a new session token on user authentication, including re-authentication, and terminates the current session token. |
| V7.4.2 | Verify that the application terminates all active sessions when a user account is disabled or deleted (such as an employee leaving the company). |
| C3.2.1 | Verify that models undergo automated input validation testing, safety evaluation testing, and output sanitization testing before deployment. |
| C11.2.1 | Verify that model-inferred sensitive attributes are not directly returned in outputs. |

## Appendix: what each requirement asks for

The words of the standard, for every requirement that applies to this app, in the order of the chapters above.

| requirement | what it asks for |
|---|---|
| V1.2.1 | Verify that output encoding for an HTTP response, HTML document, or XML document is relevant for the context required, such as encoding the relevant characters for HTML elements, HTML attributes, HTML comments, CSS, or HTTP header fields, to avoid changing the message or document structure. |
| V1.2.2 | Verify that when dynamically building URLs, untrusted data is encoded according to its context (e.g., URL encoding or base64url encoding for query or path parameters). Ensure that only safe URL protocols are permitted (e.g., disallow javascript: or data:). |
| V1.2.3 | Verify that output encoding or escaping is used when dynamically building JavaScript content (including JSON), to avoid changing the message or document structure (to avoid JavaScript and JSON injection). |
| V1.2.4 | Verify that data selection or database queries (e.g., SQL, HQL, NoSQL, Cypher) use parameterized queries, ORMs, entity frameworks, or are otherwise protected from SQL Injection and other database injection attacks. This is also relevant when writing stored procedures. |
| V1.2.5 | Verify that the application protects against OS command injection and that operating system calls use parameterized OS queries or use contextual command line output encoding. |
| V1.3.1 | Verify that all untrusted HTML input from WYSIWYG editors or similar is sanitized using a well-known and secure HTML sanitization library or framework feature. |
| V1.3.2 | Verify that the application avoids the use of eval() or other dynamic code execution features such as Spring Expression Language (SpEL). Where there is no alternative, any user input being included must be sanitized before being executed. |
| V1.1.1 | Verify that input is decoded or unescaped into a canonical form only once, it is only decoded when encoded data in that form is expected, and that this is done before processing the input further, for example it is not performed after input validation or sanitization. |
| V1.1.2 | Verify that the application performs output encoding and escaping either as a final step before being used by the interpreter for which it is intended or by the interpreter itself. |
| V1.2.9 | Verify that the application escapes special characters in regular expressions (typically using a backslash) to prevent them from being misinterpreted as metacharacters. |
| V1.3.3 | Verify that data being passed to a potentially dangerous context is sanitized beforehand to enforce safety measures, such as only allowing characters which are safe for this context and trimming input which is too long. |
| V1.3.4 | Verify that user-supplied Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) scriptable content is validated or sanitized to contain only tags and attributes (such as draw graphics) that are safe for the application, e.g., do not contain scripts and foreignObject. |
| V1.3.5 | Verify that the application sanitizes or disables user-supplied scriptable or expression template language content, such as Markdown, CSS or XSL stylesheets, BBCode, or similar. |
| V1.3.6 | Verify that the application protects against Server-side Request Forgery (SSRF) attacks, by validating untrusted data against an allowlist of protocols, domains, paths and ports and sanitizing potentially dangerous characters before using the data to call another service. |
| V1.3.7 | Verify that the application protects against template injection attacks by not allowing templates to be built based on untrusted input. Where there is no alternative, any untrusted input being included dynamically during template creation must be sanitized or strictly validated. |
| V1.4.1 | Verify that the application uses memory-safe string, safer memory copy and pointer arithmetic to detect or prevent stack, buffer, or heap overflows. |
| V1.4.2 | Verify that sign, range, and input validation techniques are used to prevent integer overflows. |
| V1.4.3 | Verify that dynamically allocated memory and resources are released, and that references or pointers to freed memory are removed or set to null to prevent dangling pointers and use-after-free vulnerabilities. |
| V1.5.2 | Verify that deserialization of untrusted data enforces safe input handling, such as using an allowlist of object types or restricting client-defined object types, to prevent deserialization attacks. Deserialization mechanisms that are explicitly defined as insecure must not be used with untrusted input. |
| V1.2.10 | Verify that the application is protected against CSV and Formula Injection. The application must follow the escaping rules defined in RFC 4180 sections 2.6 and 2.7 when exporting CSV content. Additionally, when exporting to CSV or other spreadsheet formats (such as XLS, XLSX, or ODF), special characters (including '=', '+', '-', '@', '\\t' (tab), and '\\0' (null character)) must be escaped with a single quote if they appear as the first character in a field value. |
| V1.3.12 | Verify that regular expressions are free from elements causing exponential backtracking, and ensure untrusted input is sanitized to mitigate ReDoS or Runaway Regex attacks. |
| V1.5.3 | Verify that different parsers used in the application for the same data type (e.g., JSON parsers, XML parsers, URL parsers), perform parsing in a consistent way and use the same character encoding mechanism to avoid issues such as JSON Interoperability vulnerabilities or different URI or file parsing behavior being exploited in Remote File Inclusion (RFI) or Server-side Request Forgery (SSRF) attacks. |
| V2.1.1 | Verify that the application's documentation defines input validation rules for how to check the validity of data items against an expected structure. This could be common data formats such as credit card numbers, email addresses, telephone numbers, or it could be an internal data format. |
| V2.2.1 | Verify that input is validated to enforce business or functional expectations for that input. This should either use positive validation against an allow list of values, patterns, and ranges, or be based on comparing the input to an expected structure and logical limits according to predefined rules. For L1, this can focus on input which is used to make specific business or security decisions. For L2 and up, this should apply to all input. |
| V2.2.2 | Verify that the application is designed to enforce input validation at a trusted service layer. While client-side validation improves usability and should be encouraged, it must not be relied upon as a security control. |
| V2.3.1 | Verify that the application will only process business logic flows for the same user in the expected sequential step order and without skipping steps. |
| V2.1.2 | Verify that the application's documentation defines how to validate the logical and contextual consistency of combined data items, such as checking that suburb and ZIP code match. |
| V2.1.3 | Verify that expectations for business logic limits and validations are documented, including both per-user and globally across the application. |
| V2.2.3 | Verify that the application ensures that combinations of related data items are reasonable according to the pre-defined rules. |
| V2.3.2 | Verify that business logic limits are implemented per the application's documentation to avoid business logic flaws being exploited. |
| V2.3.3 | Verify that transactions are being used at the business logic level such that either a business logic operation succeeds in its entirety or it is rolled back to the previous correct state. |
| V2.3.4 | Verify that business logic level locking mechanisms are used to ensure that limited quantity resources (such as theater seats or delivery slots) cannot be double-booked by manipulating the application's logic. |
| V2.4.1 | Verify that anti-automation controls are in place to protect against excessive calls to application functions that could lead to data exfiltration, garbage-data creation, quota exhaustion, rate-limit breaches, denial-of-service, or overuse of costly resources. |
| V2.3.5 | Verify that high-value business logic flows require multi-user approval to prevent unauthorized or accidental actions. This could include but is not limited to large monetary transfers, contract approvals, access to classified information, or safety overrides in manufacturing. |
| V2.4.2 | Verify that business logic flows require realistic human timing, preventing excessively rapid transaction submissions. |
